- The Rust Foundation’s 2025 security update says Alpha-Omega funding is supporting a Rust-focused implementation of Capslock, and the January 2026 Project Director update says that capability analyzer now has a functioning prototype. That is a strong signal that runtime-authority review is moving from theory into ecosystem infrastructure.
- The `cargo-capslock` repository already exists, FOSDEM 2026 material explicitly frames its Rust output as a path toward generated seccomp profiles for services, and `cargo-caps` already provides a second analyzer lane based on emitted symbols. That means the next missing layer is not another capability model but the artifact boundary that keeps **declared powers, inferred powers, generated enforcement, enacted enforcement, and checked behavior** distinct.
- Rust already has multiple real capability islands — `cap-std`, Wasmtime/WASI, Tauri permissions, plugin hosts, and container/runtime policy. The missing contribution is the portable review contract above them, not a universal permissions language.
- This seam composes directly with Policy / Trust / SBOM / Lifecycle work, Service Productization, Extension Productization, Client App work, and Dependency Review. It is one of the clearest ways to turn supply-chain concern into blast-radius reduction instead of only better registry metadata.

**What the contribution should look like in practice**
- Treat [`design/runtime-capability-kit.md`](./design/runtime-capability-kit.md), [`design/runtime-capability-pilot-program.md`](./design/runtime-capability-pilot-program.md), and [`proposals/epic-runtime-capability-kit.md`](./proposals/epic-runtime-capability-kit.md) as one execution band.
- Prioritize an artifact family that keeps `runtime-capability-surface`, `authority-profile`, `scope-profile`, `delegation-map`, optional `cap-inference-report`, optional `sandbox-generation-report`, optional `cap-enactment-report`, `cap-check-report`, and `cap-pack` visibly distinct.
- The next credible move is a ranked pilot program: service-binary inference first, declaration-versus-inference reconciliation second, generated-enforcement lane third, enactment-continuity lane fourth, framework-attachment lane fifth, and policy/release consumer lane sixth.
- The bar is not “a Rust seccomp generator”, “a permissions DSL”, or “a cargo-audit for syscalls”. The bar is portable least-privilege evidence that keeps declarations, analysis, candidate enforcement, enacted policy, and checked runtime behavior honest and composable.


### 4) Navigation + Commons Stack (Ecosystem Atlas + Interop Commons)
**Why this just moved into the top band**
- Rust’s vision work is unusually direct now: users need help navigating crates.io, need better “starter set” guidance, and part of the answer is smoother interop plus shared building blocks.
- The 2025 survey says docs remain canonical while LLM/editor tooling is rising, which means the ecosystem now needs guidance artifacts and shared seams that are explicit enough for both humans and tools.
- crates.io’s growing evidence surface (`pubtime`, security/advisory views, trusted-publishing posture, SLOC) means the raw inputs for better navigation are finally good enough.
- Cargo’s own development stance that plugins matter is a strong signal that a companion atlas / commons layer is the right execution posture instead of waiting for Cargo itself to become a full recommendation and interop arbiter.

**What the contribution should look like in practice**
- Treat [`design/ecosystem-atlas-kit.md`](./design/ecosystem-atlas-kit.md) and [`design/interop-commons-kit.md`](./design/interop-commons-kit.md) as a coupled band, with [`proposals/epic-ecosystem-atlas-kit.md`](./proposals/epic-ecosystem-atlas-kit.md), [`proposals/epic-interop-commons-kit.md`](./proposals/epic-interop-commons-kit.md), [`design/atlas-pilot-program.md`](./design/atlas-pilot-program.md), and [`design/interop-commons-pilot-program.md`](./design/interop-commons-pilot-program.md) as the execution anchors.
- `cargo atlas` should own domains, lanes, slot maps, curator provenance, freshness budgets, overlays, and derived guides/assistant contexts. `cargo commons` should own neutral seams, adapter truth, conformance vectors, readiness reports, and stewardship.
- The next credible move here is not universal curation. It is a paired ranked pilot program: atlas should start with CLI and HTTP/service pilots, then add client-app and safety-oriented lanes; commons should start with HTTP request/response/body and service/layer seams, then run one explicit watch/wait async borrowing/sequence readiness case so the archive proves it can refuse premature standardization honestly.
- The bar is not “Top 10 crates for X” and not “one more universal abstraction crate.” The bar is “reviewable navigation plus honest shared building blocks that make the ecosystem feel more coherent without flattening it.”

## Emerging seam just below the navigation band: Adoption Decision Stack (Atlas + Trust Decision + Maintenance Reality + Canonical Learning + Semantic Context)
**Why this now deserves explicit frontier treatment**
- Rust’s vision work says users need help navigating crates.io and do not have a clear place to get advice on a good “starter set” of crates. Atlas and Commons explain the *candidate* lanes, but the archive still lacks a project-scoped way to turn those lanes into a bounded answer for a concrete team or repo.
- The 2025 State of Rust survey says official online docs remain the canonical reference while LLM/editor tooling is rising. That means recommendation quality is now partly a **machine-consumption** problem, not only a human-curation problem.
- crates.io’s growing evidence surface, docs.rs target drift, and the Foundation’s combined emphasis on responsible adoption plus maintainer support mean the raw inputs for better decisions are finally strong enough. The missing layer is a thin consumer that keeps curator guidance, trust, sustainability, canonical docs/support posture, and local-fit evidence distinct while still making them usable together.
- Cargo’s repeated “plugins matter” stance is a strong signal that this should be a companion artifact layer, not a demand that Cargo itself become a universal recommender.

**What the contribution should look like in practice**
- Treat [`design/adoption-decision-stack.md`](./design/adoption-decision-stack.md), [`design/adoption-decision-pilot-program.md`](./design/adoption-decision-pilot-program.md), and [`proposals/epic-adoption-decision-stack.md`](./proposals/epic-adoption-decision-stack.md) as the shared execution note above [`design/ecosystem-atlas-kit.md`](./design/ecosystem-atlas-kit.md), [`design/trust-decision-stack.md`](./design/trust-decision-stack.md), [`design/maintenance-reality-stack.md`](./design/maintenance-reality-stack.md), [`design/canonical-learning-stack.md`](./design/canonical-learning-stack.md), and [`design/semantic-context-kit.md`](./design/semantic-context-kit.md).
- The next credible move is a ranked pilot program: greenfield CLI/internal-tool brief first, HTTP/service brief second, safety-oriented lane third, migration-sensitive existing-workspace brief fourth, and bounded assistant rendering fifth.
- The bar is not “an AI stack chooser,” “one more best-crates page,” or “a hidden scoring model.” The bar is “a thin adoption brief that imports lane truth, trust/maintenance posture, canonical docs, and local semantic-fit evidence with freshness and alternatives visible.”


## Emerging seam just below the navigation/adoption band: Starter Pack Kit
**Why this now deserves explicit frontier treatment**
- Rust’s 2025 vision work says users need help navigating crates.io and do not have a clear place to go for advice on a good “starter set” of crates. Atlas and Adoption Decision now address more of the *choice* problem, but the archive still lacked the next layer: turning those choices into a reviewable starter repo instead of a private pile of boilerplate.
- The 2025 State of Rust survey says online docs remain canonical while LLM/editor workflows are rising. That makes starter repos a machine-consumption surface too; ad hoc template snapshots are a poor substitute for declared provenance and freshness.
- Cargo’s built-in bootstrap remains intentionally simple, but Cargo also now inherits workspace fields during `cargo new` / `cargo init`, which means starter repos increasingly encode workspace governance and org defaults from the beginning rather than only later.
- `cargo-generate` is strong evidence that template-driven bootstrapping is real in Rust, but its discovery model also shows the current fragmentation: pre-existing git templates found through a GitHub topic rather than through a shared provenance and refresh layer.
- Rust’s 2026 flagship supply-chain work means starter repos are no longer just DX sugar. Public/private dependency posture, SBOM hooks, and related release/policy defaults increasingly begin in the starter repository.

**What the contribution should look like in practice**
- Treat [`design/starter-pack-kit.md`](./design/starter-pack-kit.md), [`design/starter-pack-pilot-program.md`](./design/starter-pack-pilot-program.md), and [`proposals/epic-starter-pack-kit.md`](./proposals/epic-starter-pack-kit.md) as the shared execution band above [`design/ecosystem-atlas-kit.md`](./design/ecosystem-atlas-kit.md), [`design/adoption-decision-stack.md`](./design/adoption-decision-stack.md), [`design/workspace-environment-stack.md`](./design/workspace-environment-stack.md), and the relevant domain productization stacks.
- Starter Pack should own starter subject, imported sources, render-plan/adapter truth, rendered layout, file ownership, overlays, freshness, and refresh reports. Atlas / Adoption Decision should keep owning lane choice. Workspace Environment should keep owning environment realization truth. Policy / Support / Release / Productization layers should remain distinct imports.
- The next credible move is the ranked rollout in [`design/starter-pack-pilot-program.md`](./design/starter-pack-pilot-program.md): conservative CLI/internal-tool starter first, HTTP/service starter second, embedded or no_std starter third, then direct-vs-delegated renderer honesty, org-overlay/refresh, and bounded guide/assistant derivations.
- The bar is not another template engine, framework quickstart wrapper, hidden starter catalog, or one giant repo generator. The bar is “a thin linked pack that lets maintainers, teams, and tools review a Rust starter repo as a **refreshable derivative of real recommendation and environment truths** instead of reconstructing it from copied files and folklore.”

### 5) Semantic Context + Edit Workflow Stack
**Why this just moved up**
- The 2025 State of Rust survey says official online docs remain the canonical reference while LLM tooling and agentic/editor workflows are increasingly part of how people learn and navigate Rust. That makes the missing problem sharper: Rust needs a trustworthy machine-usable substrate, not just more chat wrappers or editor magic.
- Cargo is actively moving toward more structured, machine-facing seams: plumbing commands, schema work for `cargo report`, and a better `cargo fix` architecture. docs.rs now hosts rustdoc JSON, and cross-crate semver work is already blocked on better feature-resolution and `rmeta`-aware combination.
- StableMIR and `rustc_public` are strong signals that the compiler side wants tool developers to have better interfaces. The missing layer is the portable evidence/report boundary that turns these raw inputs into reusable context and reviewable edits.

**What the contribution should look like in practice**
- Treat [`design/semantic-context-kit.md`](./design/semantic-context-kit.md) and [`design/edit-workflow-kit.md`](./design/edit-workflow-kit.md) as a shared tool-facing substrate band, with [`proposals/epic-semantic-context-kit.md`](./proposals/epic-semantic-context-kit.md) and [`proposals/epic-edit-workflow-kit.md`](./proposals/epic-edit-workflow-kit.md) as the execution anchors.
- `cargo semctx` should own exact subject identity, build/resolution context, semantic input provenance, merge/completeness truth, and bounded query reports. `cargo editflow` should own candidate discovery, selection, application, and verification over that context.
- The next credible move is now a paired rollout: a ranked **semantic-context pilot program** (local semver lane first, docs.rs lookup second, fix/lint handoff third, editor/assistant slice fourth, compiler-backed augmentation fifth) together with the ranked **edit-governance pilot program** (compiler suggestions first, Cargo fix / edition waves second, rust-analyzer assist / SSR export third, assistant proposals fourth, and CI/policy consumers fifth). See [`design/semantic-context-pilot-program.md`](./design/semantic-context-pilot-program.md) and [`design/edit-governance-pilot-program.md`](./design/edit-governance-pilot-program.md).
- The bar is not “an AI Rust assistant” or “one more editor plugin.” The bar is “portable semantic context and governed edit artifacts that humans, CI, IDEs, and assistants can all consume without each rebuilding a hidden partial index or bypassing review.”

## Emerging seam just below the top band: Lint Governance Stack (Lint Baseline + Compile Guidance + Edit Workflow + Policy)
**Why this now deserves explicit frontier treatment**
- Rust’s 2026 flagship plan explicitly includes **safety-critical lints in Clippy**, which makes lint posture part of assurance-facing ecosystem work rather than only convenience or style. That raises the value of portable lint evidence and reviewable debt accounting.
- Cargo has already stabilized `[lints]` and `[workspace.lints]`, so selected lint posture and inheritance are now manifest-level facts instead of only `RUSTFLAGS` folklore. Nightly Cargo is also experimenting with `[lints.cargo]`, which means Cargo-native warning families are moving toward the same policy plane.
- `cargo report` already provides a machine-usable report lane for future incompatibilities, while `cargo fix` remains useful but operationally awkward for selective/config-sensitive rollouts. The missing contribution is therefore not another linter or autofix wrapper, but the governance layer connecting **selected policy → observed findings/debt → reviewable fixes → downstream verdicts**.

**What the contribution should look like in practice**
- Treat [`design/lint-governance-stack.md`](./design/lint-governance-stack.md), [`design/lint-governance-pilot-program.md`](./design/lint-governance-pilot-program.md), [`proposals/epic-lint-governance-stack.md`](./proposals/epic-lint-governance-stack.md), [`design/lint-baseline-kit.md`](./design/lint-baseline-kit.md), [`design/compile-guidance-kit.md`](./design/compile-guidance-kit.md), [`design/edit-workflow-kit.md`](./design/edit-workflow-kit.md), and [`design/policy-kit.md`](./design/policy-kit.md) as one coupled frontier band.
- Compile Guidance should keep owning maintainer-authored lint/diagnostic catalogs and examples. Lint Baseline should keep owning selected policy, workspace inheritance, observed findings, debt baselines, and fix packs. Edit Workflow should keep owning selection/application/verification of edits. Policy should keep owning thresholds, waivers, and consumer-specific conclusions.
- The next credible move is now split into an explicit proposal layer plus the ranked pilot: [`proposals/epic-lint-governance-stack.md`](./proposals/epic-lint-governance-stack.md) should own the stack-level contract, while [`design/lint-governance-pilot-program.md`](./design/lint-governance-pilot-program.md) should still run workspace/profile-lock exports first, baseline drift and debt budgets second, fixpack/apply handoff third, Cargo future-incompat / Cargo-lint import lane fourth, and safety/release/policy consumers fifth.
- The bar is not another Clippy preset, autofix daemon, lint dashboard, or cleanliness score. The bar is portable lint-governance evidence that humans, CI, migration tooling, release review, and safety-heavy consumers can import honestly.

### 6) Compile-Time Surface Stack (Compile-Time Capabilities + Build Extension + Macro Workflow)
**Why this just moved up**
- Rust now has converging official motion on the whole compile-time surface, not just one corner of it: sandboxed build scripts are exploring per-crate permissions and a common interface with proc-macro concerns; macro-improvements work explicitly aims to reduce proc-macro demand; and the build-performance/cache work increasingly needs better input and idempotence truth.
- The compile-time frontier is no longer just “security”. It is trust, reproducibility, cacheability, build performance, macro complexity, and migration pressure all landing on the same seam.
- Cargo-script and reflection/comptime matter here too, not because they are the same thing, but because they show Rust is opening new first-class compile-time and compile-time-adjacent lanes. That raises the value of having a portable substrate instead of more folklore.

**What the contribution should look like in practice**
- Treat [`design/compile-time-capabilities-kit.md`](./design/compile-time-capabilities-kit.md), [`design/build-extension-kit.md`](./design/build-extension-kit.md), and [`design/macro-workflow-kit.md`](./design/macro-workflow-kit.md) as one strategic band, with [`design/compile-time-surface-pilot-program.md`](./design/compile-time-surface-pilot-program.md) and [`design/compile-time-profile-ladder.md`](./design/compile-time-profile-ladder.md) as the execution anchors.
- `cargo ct` should own authority, observation, determinism, policy truth, and the named profile catalog / fit reports that explain whether a subject is merely observed, declared, narrowed, sandbox-ready, no-run, or language-first. `cargo buildext` should own declarative replacement and Cargo-mediated artifact uplift. `cargo macro` should own inventory, expansion/debug/cost artifacts, and migration hints.
- The next credible move is no longer just “run the pilot program”. It is to run that pilot program against a named ladder: strict build-script authority lane → proc-macro-heavy workflow lane → structured replacement lane (`links` override / metabuild / delegation) → shareable single-file repro lane → future-facing transition lane, each with explicit graduation targets.
- The bar is not “a sandbox demo”, “a better cargo-expand”, or “a prettier build.rs wrapper”. The bar is “reviewable compile-time surfaces that Rust can progressively isolate, replace, and govern with named stages instead of folklore.”

## Emerging seam just below the top band: Compiler Extensibility Stack (MIR Analysis + Lint Governance + Compile Guidance + Conformance Traceability)
**Why this now deserves explicit frontier treatment**
- Rust’s current vision work is explicit that the ecosystem should **double down on extensibility** beyond the earliest compilation stages, and it names Stable MIR and build-std as examples of the direction.
- The StableMIR / `rustc_public` effort is trying to create a lower-maintenance compiler-facing surface for analyzers, linters, dev environments, and other tools. That makes the next missing layer less about “can we export something?” and more about “how do tools attach, declare scope, and hand results to consumers honestly?”
- `cargo-semver-checks` is the clearest practical proof: it is valuable enough to be considered for Cargo integration, but it still needs better attachment, cross-crate visibility, precise type information, and witness-generation infrastructure. The old `semverver` experience is an equally clear warning against relying on compiler-internal APIs as the long-term ecosystem contract.
- The 2026 flagships page broadens the pressure: safety-critical lints in Clippy, prototype reflection, build-std work, and cargo plumbing are all active. Cargo’s recent development notes continue to emphasize plugins and companion tools. The ecosystem therefore needs a product boundary for compiler-attached tools rather than more isolated experiments.

**What the contribution should look like in practice**
- Treat [`design/compiler-extensibility-stack.md`](./design/compiler-extensibility-stack.md), [`design/compiler-extensibility-pilot-program.md`](./design/compiler-extensibility-pilot-program.md), [`proposals/epic-compiler-extensibility-stack.md`](./proposals/epic-compiler-extensibility-stack.md), [`design/mir-analysis-kit.md`](./design/mir-analysis-kit.md), [`design/lint-governance-stack.md`](./design/lint-governance-stack.md), [`design/compile-guidance-kit.md`](./design/compile-guidance-kit.md), and [`design/conformance-traceability-stack.md`](./design/conformance-traceability-stack.md) as one coupled frontier band.
- MIR Analysis should keep owning compiler-derived semantic export. Lint Governance should keep owning selected policy, findings, debt, and fix handoff. Compile Guidance should keep owning maintainer-authored diagnostic/help/example truth. Conformance Traceability should keep owning spec/assurance consumers. The Compiler Extensibility Stack is the layer that keeps their **attachment lanes, subject/configuration truth, capability/stability posture, result families, and consumer handoffs** legible together.
- The next credible move is the ranked pilot program in [`design/compiler-extensibility-pilot-program.md`](./design/compiler-extensibility-pilot-program.md): SemVer/compatibility lane first, safety-critical lint lane second, `rustc_public`/MIR read-only analyzer lane third, guidance/fix handoff lane fourth, and assurance/conformance consumer lane fifth. The explicit proposal-layer candidate is now [`proposals/epic-compiler-extensibility-stack.md`](./proposals/epic-compiler-extensibility-stack.md), which frames the worthy contribution as a thin `cargo toolcontract` / `tool-pack` companion layer rather than a universal compiler-plugin ABI or an inevitable Cargo merger.
- The bar is not “a universal compiler plugin API,” “another nightly analyzer,” or “merge every good tool into Cargo.” The bar is a **portable contract layer for compiler-attached tools** so CI, editors, release review, policy, and safety consumers can import results honestly.

## Emerging seam just below the top band: Reflection Transition Stack
**Why this now deserves first-class treatment in the archive**
- Rust's 2026 flagship work explicitly includes **prototype reflection**, which makes reflection an active language-adjacent frontier rather than a niche library trick or a far-future thought experiment.
- The reflection-and-comptime goal says proc-macro derives have historically been hard to debug and bootstrap, proposes a compile-time reflection path, and also says crates like `bevy_reflect` and `facet` would still exist afterwards with different goals. That makes ecosystem comparison and migration the real missing seam.
- The August 2025 program-management update makes the userland pressure concrete: Bevy and gamedev users raised reflection as a major pain point, the writeup explains why derive-based reflection is difficult to write/debug, and it calls out orphan-rule friction for standard-library and foreign types. Reflection transition is therefore not just a language-design question; it is an ecosystem migration problem.
- Macro-improvements and compiler-performance work sharpen the same point from another direction: reducing proc-macro demand is explicitly good for build speed, dependency size, and debugging quality. That means today's macro burden belongs inside the reflection story.
- The ecosystem already has several legitimate but non-equivalent lanes — runtime reflection, associated-const shape metadata, object-safe inspection, schema tracing, and proc-macro-heavy derives — so the missing contribution is a transition boundary above them, not a decree that one lane has already won.

**What the contribution should look like in practice**
- Treat [`design/reflection-surface-kit.md`](./design/reflection-surface-kit.md), [`design/macro-workflow-kit.md`](./design/macro-workflow-kit.md), and [`design/const-surface-kit.md`](./design/const-surface-kit.md) as one coupled execution band, with [`design/reflection-transition-stack.md`](./design/reflection-transition-stack.md) and [`design/reflection-transition-pilot-program.md`](./design/reflection-transition-pilot-program.md) as the synthesis / rollout layer above them.
- Reflection Surface should own acquisition/shape/access/registry/adapter truth. Macro Workflow should own today's proc-macro inventory/cost/debug/migration burden. Const Surface should own compile-time capability/cost/fallback truth for reflection-adjacent lanes. Reflection Transition should keep **lane identity, comparison, migration posture, foreign-type/orphan-rule pressure, watch/wait outcomes, and bounded consumer handoffs** explicit above them.
- The next credible move is now explicit: pair the ranked pilot program with [`proposals/epic-reflection-transition-stack.md`](./proposals/epic-reflection-transition-stack.md), a thin `cargo reflect-transition` / `reflection-transition-pack/v0` layer above Macro Workflow + Reflection Surface + Const Surface. The rollout should start with visit-only observability lanes, then runtime registry/editor lanes, then compile-time shape/schema comparisons, then derive-heavy migration reports, and only after that widen to future core-reflection comparisons.
- The bar is not “a universal reflect trait”, “a new registry macro”, “another derive helper”, or “wait until language reflection lands”. The bar is “reviewable transition artifacts that let the ecosystem compare and migrate reflection lanes honestly without flattening runtime metadata, visit-only visitors, schema traces, proc-macro burden, orphan-rule coverage, and future compile-time reflection into one fake story.”

### 7) Resource Evidence Stack (Benchmark Evidence + Perf Labs + Footprint Kit)
**Why this now belongs in the top band**
- The pain is broader than benchmarking alone. The 2025 State of Rust survey still lists resource usage — especially slow compile times and storage usage — among the biggest productivity problems, and the 2024 survey separately called out high disk usage of compiler artifacts. That means the frontier is not just “perf tooling”; it is a missing substrate for **time + storage + footprint truth**.
- Official Rust/Cargo motion is converging on exactly those surfaces: Cargo build-analysis and `cargo report` work make compile-workflow evidence more machine-usable, the build-dir-layout work explicitly targets GC and future shared caches, and compiler-performance work keeps emphasizing workflow diversity instead of one simple “Rust is slow” story.
- Rust already has strong point tools for runtime benchmarking, native-size analysis, Wasm retained-size analysis, stack analysis, and allocator profiling. The missing contribution is the attachable review layer above them, not one more runner or shrinker.

**What the contribution should look like in practice**
- Treat [`design/benchmark-evidence-kit.md`](./design/benchmark-evidence-kit.md), [`design/benchmark-evidence-pilot-program.md`](./design/benchmark-evidence-pilot-program.md), [`design/perf-labs.md`](./design/perf-labs.md), [`design/perf-pilot-program.md`](./design/perf-pilot-program.md), [`design/footprint-kit.md`](./design/footprint-kit.md), [`design/resource-evidence-pilot-program.md`](./design/resource-evidence-pilot-program.md), [`proposals/epic-benchmark-evidence-kit.md`](./proposals/epic-benchmark-evidence-kit.md), [`proposals/epic-perf-labs.md`](./proposals/epic-perf-labs.md), and [`proposals/epic-footprint-kit.md`](./proposals/epic-footprint-kit.md) as one strategic band.
- `cargo benchpack` should keep owning benchmark-native subject ids, lane/counter semantics, runner imports, baseline imports, and result/attachment lineage. `cargo perf` should keep owning broader performance-subject identity, cross-workload comparison, collector profiles, baseline freshness, and reason-coded gates above imported benchmark packs and compile/service lanes. `cargo footprint` should keep owning explicit size/section/stack/allocation budgets, measurement coverage, and diffable resource reports. A later `resource-evidence-pack/v0` can bundle linked packs when a review genuinely needs multiple families.
- The next credible move is a ranked shared pilot program: stable benchmark-native lanes first, compile-workflow + build-storage evidence second, native release-artifact lanes third, constrained embedded/Wasm lanes fourth, runtime allocation lanes fifth, and federated resource-review consumers last.
- The bar is not “another benchmark crate”, “another size tool”, or “a universal resource dashboard”. The bar is “reviewable benchmark, performance, and footprint evidence that CI, release review, and downstream tooling can consume honestly without flattening time, counters, storage, artifact bytes, and memory into one fake score.”

### 8) Safety-Critical Evidence Stack (Safety Evidence + Coverage Evidence + Sanitizer Battery + Formal Verification)
**Why this belongs in the top band**
- Rust’s 2026 flagship roadmap is unusually explicit here: Safety-Critical Rust is now defined in terms of MC/DC, normative `unsafe` documentation, safety-critical Clippy work, and FLS release cadence rather than generic aspirations.
- The biggest missing piece is not merely another verifier. It is the reviewable evidence layer that keeps unsafe contracts, coverage criteria, runtime-checking lanes, and proof assumptions distinct while still letting a reviewer assemble one honest case.
- This is also one of the few places where Rust can differentiate by making assurance *structured* instead of bespoke.

**What the contribution should look like in practice**
- Treat [`design/safety-critical-evidence-stack.md`](./design/safety-critical-evidence-stack.md), [`design/safety-critical-pilot-program.md`](./design/safety-critical-pilot-program.md), [`proposals/epic-safety-critical-evidence-stack.md`](./proposals/epic-safety-critical-evidence-stack.md), [`design/safety-evidence-kit.md`](./design/safety-evidence-kit.md), [`design/coverage-evidence-kit.md`](./design/coverage-evidence-kit.md), [`design/coverage-evidence-pilot-program.md`](./design/coverage-evidence-pilot-program.md), [`design/sanitizer-battery-kit.md`](./design/sanitizer-battery-kit.md), [`design/formal-verification-kit.md`](./design/formal-verification-kit.md), and the new [`design/formal-verification-pilot-program.md`](./design/formal-verification-pilot-program.md) as one strategic band.
- The next credible move is now three-part and explicit: first harden criterion-aware coverage through the ranked coverage pilot (LLVM baseline → nextest+doctest merge → external/FFI merge → Tarpaulin contrast → criterion-aware safety import), harden proof/report interoperability through the ranked formal-verification pilot (Cargo-native pair across unlike families → replay/counterexample lane → std-contract/library slice → multi-backend diff → safety-critical import), and only then compose a thin `cargo safety-critical` / `safety-critical-pack/v0` layer above case assembly, criterion-aware coverage, dynamic-analysis lanes, and proof assumptions through the broader shared pilot program.
- Keep unsafe-contract authority, coverage criteria, sanitizer capability/target truth, proof assumptions, and certification-facing handoffs distinct instead of treating them as one universal “assurance ready” claim.
- The bar is not “one more safety checklist” and not “Rust certification in a box.” The bar is “attachable evidence other teams, auditors, and high-assurance adopters can actually reason about without flattening it into one fake assurance score.”

### 9) Debuggability Stack (Diagnostic Surface + Observability + Debugger Experience)
**Why this now belongs in the top band**
- The 2025 State of Rust survey still puts debugging among the leading productivity problems, says online docs remain canonical, and notes that many users really do find compiler error explanations useful. That is a strong signal that better debugging starts with trustworthy failure surfaces, not only with better debuggers.
- The 2026 debugging survey makes the missing contract explicit: support varies across debugger families/versions, operating systems, visualizers, async debugging, and expression evaluation. The ecosystem still tends to collapse all of that into one vague “debugging works” claim.
- Cargo’s structured-report direction (`cargo report timings`, `rebuild`, `sessions`) and Rust’s recent vision work on supportive interfaces make this a good time to design the shared evidence layer above diagnostics, telemetry, and debugger tuple claims rather than another isolated point tool.

**What the contribution should look like in practice**
- Treat [`design/debuggability-stack.md`](./design/debuggability-stack.md), [`design/debuggability-pilot-program.md`](./design/debuggability-pilot-program.md), [`design/diagnostic-surface-kit.md`](./design/diagnostic-surface-kit.md), [`design/observability-kit.md`](./design/observability-kit.md), [`design/debugger-experience-kit.md`](./design/debugger-experience-kit.md), [`design/debugger-pilot-program.md`](./design/debugger-pilot-program.md), and [`proposals/epic-debuggability-stack.md`](./proposals/epic-debuggability-stack.md) as one strategic band.
- The next credible move is now explicit: a thin `cargo debuggability` / `debuggability-pack/v0` layer above diagnostics, runtime correlation, debugger tuple truth, and replay/incident handoff, proven through a ranked shared pilot program: failure-identity lane first, debugger tuple-truth lane second, runtime-correlation lane third, repro/replay handoff lane fourth, and support/compatibility consumers fifth.
- Keep declared diagnostic truth, runtime/telemetry truth, debugger tuple truth, and replay/incident handoff truth distinct instead of treating them as one universal “debugging support” claim.
- The bar is not “a better IDE plugin”, “another tracing bootstrap”, or “one more error-reporting crate”. The bar is “portable, testable debuggability evidence that can escalate honestly from failure identity to runtime correlation to tuple-specific debugger support.”

### 10) Native Edge Stack (FFI Boundary + Native Dependency)
**Why this now belongs in the top band**
- The Rust project is explicitly treating C++/Rust interop as a real adoption problem, first by evaluating seamless interop approaches and then by launching a problem-space mapping effort. That means mixed-language adoption is no longer just external ecosystem chatter.
- Cargo is simultaneously trying to reduce bespoke build-script behavior, experiment with plumbing, and make delegated / artifact-driven build flows more structured. That makes boundary and native-provider evidence unusually timely.
- The Rust Foundation's current strategy emphasizes responsible growth in adoption and engagement from organizations that rely on Rust. The native edge is one of the sharpest places where that pressure becomes operational.
- Rust already has strong point tools (`bindgen`, `cbindgen`, `cxx`, `autocxx`, `pkg-config`, `system-deps`, `vcpkg`, `cmake`). The missing contribution is the attachable contract and handoff layer, not one more generator or provider shim.

**What the contribution should look like in practice**
- Treat [`gaps/native-edge-adoption-boundaries-provider-locks-and-build-handoffs.md`](./gaps/native-edge-adoption-boundaries-provider-locks-and-build-handoffs.md), [`design/native-edge-stack.md`](./design/native-edge-stack.md), [`design/native-edge-pilot-program.md`](./design/native-edge-pilot-program.md), [`design/ffi-boundary-kit.md`](./design/ffi-boundary-kit.md), and [`design/native-dependency-kit.md`](./design/native-dependency-kit.md) as one coupled frontier band.
- `cargo ffi` should keep owning explicit boundary manifests, generated artifacts, drift reports, and downstream-consumable packs. `cargo native` should keep owning native intent, provider locks, link plans, reason-coded reports, and provider-aware bundles. The Native Edge Stack is the thin joined review boundary above them.
- The explicit proposal-layer candidate is now [`proposals/epic-native-edge-stack.md`](./proposals/epic-native-edge-stack.md): a thin `cargo native-edge` / `native-edge-pack/v0` layer above FFI Boundary + Native Dependency, with ranked rollout still driven by [`design/native-edge-pilot-program.md`](./design/native-edge-pilot-program.md): C ABI export first, system-library consumers second, C++ bridge lanes third, external build handoff fourth, and support/audit consumers fifth.
- The bar is not “another FFI helper” and not “a Rust-native package manager for C libraries.” The bar is “portable boundary and native-provider evidence that large mixed-language codebases and external build systems can actually consume.”

### 11) Maintenance Reality Stack (Lifecycle Ledger + Stewardship Ops)
**Why this now belongs in the frontier band**
- The Rust Foundation’s current strategy makes **Sustainable Maintenance** a core pillar, and the Maintainers Fund plus annual-report language make maintainer support a strategic Rust concern rather than background community rhetoric.
- Official Rust writing is increasingly explicit that triage, review routing, backports, documentation upkeep, meeting/process followthrough, and contributor intake are part of how Rust ships. That means maintenance operations now look like infrastructure, not just sociology.
- Forge procedures and triagebot flows already encode enough real workflow semantics — labels, aging thresholds, prioritization steps, alerts, backports — that a companion artifact/export layer can start narrow and useful instead of speculative.
- This stack also complements stronger frontier bands instead of competing with them: Atlas, Policy, Release Pipeline, and Trust Signals all benefit when maintenance reality becomes importable evidence instead of folklore.

**What the contribution should look like in practice**
- Treat [`design/lifecycle-ledger-kit.md`](./design/lifecycle-ledger-kit.md), [`design/stewardship-ops-kit.md`](./design/stewardship-ops-kit.md), [`design/stewardship-pilot-program.md`](./design/stewardship-pilot-program.md), [`design/maintenance-reality-stack.md`](./design/maintenance-reality-stack.md), and [`proposals/epic-maintenance-reality-stack.md`](./proposals/epic-maintenance-reality-stack.md) as one coupled maintenance band. The stack now has an explicit epic candidate: a thin `cargo maintenance-reality` / `maintenance-reality-pack/v0` layer above Lifecycle Ledger + Stewardship Ops rather than only the lower-level `cargo lifecycle` and `cargo steward` lanes.
- Lifecycle Ledger should keep owning declared support windows, deprecations, successors, and handoff consent. Stewardship Ops should keep owning queue policy, snapshots, pressure reports, help requests, mentoring lanes, action logs, and support-routing posture.
- The next credible move is a ranked rollout: PR-triage exporter first, issue-intake/repro lane second, release/backport readiness third, maintainer-help routing fourth, and mentoring-capacity lane fifth.
- The bar is not “a maintainer dashboard” or “a health score.” The bar is “portable maintenance truth that recommendation, governance, release, and support systems can consume honestly, with explicit public-vs-restricted boundaries.”


### 12) Service Productization Stack (Service Surface + Runtime Settings + Observability + Async Reliability + Background Work + Support Envelope)
**Why this now deserves frontier treatment**
- The 2024 State of Rust survey says Rust is especially popular for **server backends, web and networking services, and cloud technologies**, and company usage keeps rising. That means the missing problem is increasingly productization quality, not just framework availability.
- The 2024 and 2025 surveys keep **resource usage, debugging, and async/tooling friction** in the pain set, which is where real services hurt once the demo works.
- The 2026 flagship themes keep **Just Add Async** active, while rustdoc `doc_cfg` and docs.rs target/default changes make support and availability truth more important for service users and operators.
- The ecosystem already has credible building blocks for routing, config, telemetry, jobs, and runtime control. The missing contribution is therefore not another framework, but a portable layer that makes those surfaces compose honestly.

**What the contribution should look like in practice**
- Treat [`design/service-productization-stack.md`](./design/service-productization-stack.md), [`design/service-productization-pilot-program.md`](./design/service-productization-pilot-program.md), [`design/service-surface-kit.md`](./design/service-surface-kit.md), [`design/runtime-settings-kit.md`](./design/runtime-settings-kit.md), [`design/observability-kit.md`](./design/observability-kit.md), [`design/async-reliability-stack.md`](./design/async-reliability-stack.md), [`design/background-work-kit.md`](./design/background-work-kit.md), and [`design/support-envelope-kit.md`](./design/support-envelope-kit.md) as one coupled frontier band.
- Service Surface should keep owning request/response and middleware truth. Runtime Settings should keep owning config/default/source/secret truth. Observability should keep owning telemetry profile/report truth. Async Reliability should keep owning lifecycle/replay/escalation truth. Background Work should keep owning jobs/workflows. Support Envelope and DocProof should keep owning support/docs/runtime-floor truth.
- The next credible move is a ranked pilot program: request/config lane first, telemetry/runtime-correlation lane second, graceful shutdown and background-work lane third, support/docs lane fourth, and release/policy/incident consumers fifth.
- The bar is not another framework, app template, generated control plane, or giant platform SDK. The bar is portable service-productization evidence that humans, CI, release review, operators, and incident responders can import honestly.

## Emerging seam just below the service/agent band: Workflow Productization Stack
**Why this now deserves explicit frontier treatment**
- The archive already had a serious **Background Work Kit**, but current Rust ecosystem motion now makes the higher layer obvious: projects increasingly need a **workflow-as-product boundary** above jobs, schedules, retries, timers, journals, recovery, release lineage, and support folklore.
- The 2024 State of Rust survey says Rust is especially popular for server backends, web/networking services, and cloud technologies, which is exactly where background work and durable execution become customer-visible behavior rather than a hidden implementation detail.
- Rust now has clearly non-equivalent workflow lanes: `apalis` spans distributed backends, persisted cron jobs, worker monitoring, and graceful shutdown; `apalis-workflow` adds durable/resumable sequential and DAG workflows; `fang` adds scheduled/periodic/unique tasks with retries; `sqlxmq` makes transactional queueing, checkpointing, and consistent backup/restore the point of the lane; Restate Rust makes durable handlers, timers, promises, and journal-backed retries first-class; and Temporal’s Rust Core SDK is outside-in evidence that workflow machinery is state-machine-heavy enough to need durable product boundaries.
- That combination means the missing contribution is no longer just a lower-level work-surface kit. The worthy move is now the product layer that keeps declared work truth, runtime activation, history/recovery evidence, release lineage, and support truth distinct while making them compose.

**What the contribution should look like in practice**
- Treat [`design/workflow-productization-stack.md`](./design/workflow-productization-stack.md), [`design/workflow-productization-pilot-program.md`](./design/workflow-productization-pilot-program.md), [`proposals/epic-workflow-productization-stack.md`](./proposals/epic-workflow-productization-stack.md), [`design/background-work-kit.md`](./design/background-work-kit.md), [`design/runtime-settings-kit.md`](./design/runtime-settings-kit.md), [`design/observability-kit.md`](./design/observability-kit.md), [`design/release-truth-stack.md`](./design/release-truth-stack.md), and [`design/support-envelope-kit.md`](./design/support-envelope-kit.md) as one coupled frontier band.
- Background Work should keep owning job/workflow identities, trigger maps, schedules, retries, idempotency, and checkpoints. Runtime Settings should keep owning engine/store/retention activation. Observability should keep owning history/journal/retry/recovery evidence. Release Truth should keep owning worker/runtime/source lineage for long-lived executions. Support Envelope and DocProof should keep owning what is actually promised. Service, Agent, Event, and Identity should remain importing consumers rather than becoming new workflow authorities.
- The next credible move is now explicit: a thin `cargo workflow-product` / `workflow-product-pack/v0` layer above **Background Work + Runtime Settings + Observability + Release Truth + Support Envelope**, rolled out in ranked order — queue/schedule truth first, transactional DB-backed truth second, DAG/resumable workflow truth third, durable-engine/history truth fourth, and release/support/incident consumers fifth.
- The bar is not another queue framework, cron helper, workflow UI, hosted orchestration platform, or universal workflow DSL. The bar is portable boring workflow-product evidence that keeps declared work truth, runtime activation, history truth, release lineage, and downstream conclusions separate while making them composable.

### Emerging seam just below the service/debuggability band: Observability Productization Stack
**Why this now deserves explicit frontier treatment**
- The archive already had a strong Observability Kit, but current ecosystem signals make the next synthesis obvious: projects increasingly need an **observability-as-product boundary** above spans, metrics, logs, runtime probes, exporter defaults, and docs/setup folklore.
- `tracing-subscriber` and `tracing-log` make it clear that Rust observability is already layered and bridge-heavy rather than singular. OpenTelemetry Rust now documents traces, metrics, and logs together, but all three remain Beta in the language docs, while the OpenTelemetry project itself is explicitly prioritizing stability, release discipline, and “observability by design”.
- Tokio Console proves that runtime diagnostics are valuable enough to have a protocol, subscriber layer, and explicit activation gates. Cargo’s structured reporting work shows that attachable machine-facing runtime/build evidence is becoming more realistic inside Rust tooling.
- That combination points to a missing contribution above the lower-layer kit: **diagnostic identity truth, signal/profile truth, activation/export truth, support/docs truth, and release/incident consumer imports**.

**What the contribution should look like in practice**
- Treat [`design/observability-productization-stack.md`](./design/observability-productization-stack.md), [`design/observability-productization-pilot-program.md`](./design/observability-productization-pilot-program.md), [`design/diagnostic-surface-kit.md`](./design/diagnostic-surface-kit.md), [`design/observability-kit.md`](./design/observability-kit.md), [`design/runtime-settings-kit.md`](./design/runtime-settings-kit.md), and [`design/support-envelope-kit.md`](./design/support-envelope-kit.md) as one coupled frontier band.
- Diagnostic Surface should keep owning failure/event identity. Observability should keep owning signal/profile/report/vector truth. Runtime Settings should keep owning exporter/endpoint/sampling/redaction/runtime activation. Support Envelope and DocProof should keep owning support/docs/runtime-floor truth. Release / Incident / Debuggability should remain importing consumers.
- The next credible move is a ranked pilot program: tracing/log identity lane first, OTLP/schema/profile lane second, runtime-diagnostic lane third, support/release lane fourth, and incident/debug/domain consumers fifth.
- The bar is not another exporter wrapper, vendor bootstrap, telemetry dashboard, or runtime probe. The bar is portable observability-productization evidence that humans, CI, release review, support, and incident/debug consumers can import honestly.

### 13) Client Productization Stack (Client App Surface + A11yKit + Localization Surface + Distribution Contract + Support Envelope)
**Why this now deserves frontier treatment**
- Rust now has multiple credible client-app lanes instead of one fragile dream: Tauri 2 is stable across major desktop and mobile targets, Dioxus documents desktop/mobile setup plus distribution guidance, and Slint explicitly targets embedded, desktop, and mobile.
- Accessibility and localization are no longer optional “later” concerns for serious apps, and Rust now has real substrate pieces: AccessKit already spans multiple Rust toolkits, while Fluent / ICU4X / app-level embedding paths make locale/runtime-data truth a real design surface.
- The ecosystem’s remaining weakness is productization glue, not only toolkit existence. `cargo-mobile2` explicitly scopes its maintenance around the Tauri use case, which is a strong signal that the mobile/client story still lacks a broader portable contract layer.
- docs/support truth is also moving under real ecosystems: `doc_cfg` and docs.rs target-default changes mean app support claims need better structured evidence than README prose and one successful simulator run.

- Treat [`design/client-productization-stack.md`](./design/client-productization-stack.md), [`design/client-productization-pilot-program.md`](./design/client-productization-pilot-program.md), [`proposals/epic-client-productization-stack.md`](./proposals/epic-client-productization-stack.md), [`design/client-app-surface-kit.md`](./design/client-app-surface-kit.md), [`design/a11ykit-ui-testing.md`](./design/a11ykit-ui-testing.md), [`design/localization-surface-kit.md`](./design/localization-surface-kit.md), [`design/distribution-contract-stack.md`](./design/distribution-contract-stack.md), and [`design/support-envelope-kit.md`](./design/support-envelope-kit.md) as one coupled frontier band.
- Client App Surface should keep owning app identities, bridges, capabilities, lifecycle, and package/store profiles. A11yKit should keep owning semantic UI truth and query-driven UI testing. Localization Surface should keep owning locales, catalogs, fallback, and runtime-data posture. Distribution Contract should keep owning channel/fallback/verification/install receipts. Support Envelope and DocProof should keep owning target/runtime/docs support truth.
- The next credible move is now explicit: turn the ranked pilot program into the epic sketched in [`proposals/epic-client-productization-stack.md`](./proposals/epic-client-productization-stack.md), with app-surface/package lane first, support/docs lane second, accessibility lane third, localization lane fourth, and distribution/install plus rollout consumers fifth.
- The bar is not another GUI toolkit, shell, app template, bridge generator, or deployment SaaS. The bar is portable client-productization evidence that humans, CI, release engineering, support, and ecosystem guidance can import honestly.

### 14) Data Productization Stack (Database Contract + Schema Contract + Migration Truth + Runtime Settings + Support Envelope)
Why this now deserves frontier treatment:
- Rust’s current usage remains heavily real-world/backend/service-facing, which means stateful data work is common enough that the archive should stop treating it as a side note.
- The ecosystem already demonstrates multiple legitimate data source-of-truth families: SQLx checked/offline query evidence, Diesel schema-as-code, SeaORM/refinery migration lanes, and testcontainers-style ephemeral integration environments.
- The missing ecosystem contribution is not a new ORM winner, but a portable boring-data substrate that can keep query evidence, schema truth, migration truth, runtime activation, and support claims distinct while still letting them compose.

What the contribution should look like in practice:
- treat `design/data-productization-stack.md`, `design/data-productization-pilot-program.md`, [`proposals/epic-data-productization-stack.md`](./proposals/epic-data-productization-stack.md), `design/database-contract-kit.md`, `design/schema-contract-kit.md`, and migration/runtime/support consumers as one frontier band;
- keep ownership boundaries explicit: Database Contract = live relational truth, Schema Contract = public contract truth, Migration Truth = source→destination choreography, Runtime Settings = activation truth, Support Envelope/DocProof = promise/support truth;
- the next credible move is now explicit: a thin `cargo data-product` / `data-product-pack/v0` layer above **Database Contract + Schema Contract + Migration Truth + Runtime Settings + Support Envelope**, with the ranked execution lanes still running query/offline/docs.rs truth → migration/test-environment evidence → runtime activation → service/client schema attachments → release/support/incident consumers;
- set the bar against another ORM wrapper, query DSL, migration generator, hosted control plane, or fake universal data-platform schema.


### Emerging seam just below the data band: Search Productization Stack
**Why this now deserves frontier treatment**
- Rust’s search ecosystem is now clearly plural in the way that usually predicts a missing product boundary rather than a missing engine. Tantivy still represents the embedded/library lane, Quickwit makes index/doc-mapping/search-settings truth explicit for distributed search, LanceDB now openly positions itself for vector/full-text/hybrid retrieval applications, Qdrant treats dense+sparse fusion as a first-class operation, and Meilisearch now treats embedder-backed AI search as a documented product capability.
- The product questions these tools leave behind are increasingly the same even when the engines are not: what corpus is actually being searched, which query/facet/highlight/ranking semantics are part of the promise, how fresh the indexed data is allowed to be, which semantic/hybrid/reranker features were really active, and what support/release/agent consumers may safely conclude.
- Retrieval Surface Kit already names the query/ranking half of the problem, but current ecosystem motion makes it timely to promote the higher layer above it. The worthy contribution is no longer just a retrieval contract; it is the product boundary that keeps search semantics, corpus freshness, runtime activation, evidence, and support claims distinct while letting them compose.

**What the contribution should look like in practice**
- Treat [`design/search-productization-stack.md`](./design/search-productization-stack.md), [`design/search-productization-pilot-program.md`](./design/search-productization-pilot-program.md), [`proposals/epic-search-productization-stack.md`](./proposals/epic-search-productization-stack.md), [`design/retrieval-surface-kit.md`](./design/retrieval-surface-kit.md), [`design/dataset-surface-kit.md`](./design/dataset-surface-kit.md), [`design/runtime-settings-kit.md`](./design/runtime-settings-kit.md), [`design/observability-kit.md`](./design/observability-kit.md), and [`design/support-envelope-kit.md`](./design/support-envelope-kit.md) as one coupled frontier band.
- Retrieval Surface should keep owning corpora/query/ranking/filter/facet/highlight truth. Dataset Surface should keep owning source-data/import/freshness truth. Runtime Settings should keep owning live engine/profile/embedder/reranker activation. Observability should keep owning relevance/latency/freshness/degraded-mode evidence. Support Envelope and DocProof should keep owning what is actually promised. Service Surface, Model Surface, Identity Surface, and Schema Contract should remain importing attachment lanes rather than becoming new search authorities.
- The next credible move is now explicit: a thin `cargo search-product` / `search-product-pack/v0` layer above **Retrieval Surface + Dataset Surface + Runtime Settings + Observability + Support Envelope**, rolled out in ranked order — query/ranking truth first, corpus freshness/import truth second, semantic/hybrid/model attachments third, runtime/evidence/fallback truth fourth, and service/support/release/agent consumers fifth.
- The bar is not another search engine, vector DB wrapper, RAG framework, query dashboard, or magical hybrid-search abstraction. The bar is portable boring search-product evidence that keeps query behavior, corpus freshness, semantic attachments, runtime activation, evidence, and downstream conclusions separate while making them composable.


## Emerging seam just below the service/data band: Operator Productization Stack
**Why this now deserves frontier treatment**
- Kubernetes still defines operators as software extensions built around custom resources and control loops, so the upstream product model here is unusually crisp: CRDs, status, capabilities, install/update behavior, and support truth are all part of the subject, not implementation trivia.
- Rust’s operator lane is now broad enough that the missing contribution looks like a product boundary rather than a missing client. `kube` openly packages a controller runtime, CRD derive, and related tooling, while kube-rs controller docs now span admission, testing, observability, security, scaling, and availability.
- The testing story makes the gap sharper, not softer. kube-rs is explicit that there is no `envtest` equivalent in Rust so far, which means operator install/test/support truth is still too dependent on bespoke cluster setup and local conventions.
- Typed CRDs plus CEL-validation-aware schema generation are already live through `kube-derive` / `KubeSchema`, so the ecosystem now needs a way to keep schema truth distinct from runtime/install/support truth instead of silently merging them.
- Real operator product families already exist. Stackable’s current operator docs publish supported product versions, monitoring posture, shared CRDs, secret/certificate handling, and internal-versus-product operator roles. That is exactly the kind of evidence that should become more portable.
- The comparison with Go sharpens the seam further: `controller-runtime` already treats managers, controllers, clients/caches, schemes, webhooks, logging/metrics, and testing as one mainstream lane. Rust has serious ingredients, but not yet the reviewable product layer above them.

**What the contribution should look like in practice**
- Treat [`design/operator-productization-stack.md`](./design/operator-productization-stack.md), [`design/operator-productization-pilot-program.md`](./design/operator-productization-pilot-program.md), [`proposals/epic-operator-productization-stack.md`](./proposals/epic-operator-productization-stack.md), [`design/schema-contract-kit.md`](./design/schema-contract-kit.md), [`design/runtime-capability-kit.md`](./design/runtime-capability-kit.md), [`design/service-surface-kit.md`](./design/service-surface-kit.md), [`design/observability-kit.md`](./design/observability-kit.md), [`design/distribution-contract-stack.md`](./design/distribution-contract-stack.md), and [`design/support-envelope-kit.md`](./design/support-envelope-kit.md) as one coupled frontier band.
- Schema Contract should keep owning CRD/version/spec/status/condition truth. Runtime Capability should keep owning RBAC/resources/external-power posture. Service Surface should keep owning webhook/health/admin endpoint behavior. Observability should keep owning reconcile/status/event/metric evidence. Distribution Contract + Support Envelope/DocProof should keep owning install/upgrade/Kubernetes-version/runtime/docs truth. Policy, Conformance Traceability, Release Truth, and Incident lanes should remain importing consumers rather than becoming new operator authorities.
- The next credible move is now explicit: a thin `cargo operator-product` / `operator-product-pack/v0` layer above **Schema Contract + Runtime Capability + Service Surface + Observability + Distribution Contract + Support Envelope**, rolled out in ranked order — CRD/capability truth first, status/runtime evidence second, webhook/health surfaces third, install/upgrade/Kubernetes-version truth fourth, and release/policy/support/incident consumers fifth.
- The bar is not another controller runtime, YAML generator, Helm replacement, or hosted cluster platform. The bar is portable boring operator-product evidence that keeps CRD/schema truth, capability posture, webhook surfaces, runtime evidence, install/support truth, and downstream conclusions separate while making them composable.

The worthy contribution is no longer just a geospatial contract; it is the product boundary that keeps spatial semantics, publication/freshness, runtime activation, imported client/search/service lanes, evidence, and support claims distinct while letting them compose.

### 15) Identity Productization Stack (Identity Surface + Runtime Settings + Credentials + Support Envelope)
**Why this now deserves frontier treatment**
- The archive has already promoted service, client, and data productization as frontier seams. Identity is the cross-cutting product boundary those stacks keep importing without yet owning.
- The 2024 State of Rust survey says Rust is especially popular for server backends, web/networking services, and cloud technologies, and that employers value Rust for helping them build relatively correct and bug-free software. That is exactly the setting where auth behavior stops being framework glue and becomes part of the supported interface.
- The 2025 survey says online documentation remains the preferred canonical reference while debugging/resource friction remains real. That makes machine-readable identity/support truth more valuable than another pile of setup prose.
- AreWeWebYet’s auth topic still reads like a mixed crate catalog rather than a shared support model, which is a strong signal that the missing problem is now the portable contract layer above the ingredients.
- The ecosystem already has credible building blocks at multiple layers: `tower-sessions` for pluggable sessions, `axum-login` for user identification/authentication/authorization middleware, `oauth2` and `openidconnect` for federation/authentication, `webauthn-rs` for passkeys/WebAuthn, `tower-http` and `axum-extra` for request auth and cookie/key seams, and `cedar-policy`, `oso`, and `casbin` for different authorization models.

**What the contribution should look like in practice**
- Treat [`design/identity-productization-stack.md`](./design/identity-productization-stack.md), [`design/identity-productization-pilot-program.md`](./design/identity-productization-pilot-program.md), [`proposals/epic-identity-productization-stack.md`](./proposals/epic-identity-productization-stack.md), and [`design/identity-surface-kit.md`](./design/identity-surface-kit.md) as one execution band.
- Identity Surface should remain the anchor for supported methods, principal/claim models, and access-flow evidence. Runtime Settings and Credentials should remain the activation/secret lanes. Support Envelope and DocProof should remain the support/docs lanes. Service Surface, Command Surface, Schema Contract, and Client App Surface should remain importing attachment lanes instead of becoming new identity authorities.
- The next credible move is now explicit: turn the ranked pilot program into the epic sketched in [`proposals/epic-identity-productization-stack.md`](./proposals/epic-identity-productization-stack.md), with supported-auth/principal truth first, protected-surface attachments second, provider/cookie/session/key activation third, support/docs/browser/runtime-floor truth fourth, and transition/release/support/policy consumers fifth.
- The bar is not another auth framework, one true JWT/session abstraction, route-guard pile, or policy-engine winner pitch. The bar is portable boring identity evidence that keeps auth surface, protected surfaces, runtime activation, authorization attachments, support truth, and transition truth separate while making them composable.

### 16) Toolchain Productization Stack (Cross Toolchain + Sysroot Pack + Sanitizer Battery)
**Why this now deserves frontier treatment**
- Official Rust work is no longer treating custom or instrumented std builds as fringe. The `build-std` goal is explicitly about a stabilizable MVP, Cargo’s current unstable docs still frame `-Z build-std` as early-stage and nightly-bound, and the 2026 flagships page keeps sanitizer infrastructure active.
- rustup’s model makes the missing boundary more visible, not less: toolchains, components, targets, profiles, and overrides are separate surfaces, and rustup’s cross-compilation docs explicitly say target stdlib installation is not enough because linkers and SDKs are often still required.
- The sanitizer-support goal is especially clarifying: it explicitly says MemorySanitizer and ThreadSanitizer need infrastructure to provide **precompiled and instrumented standard libraries**. That turns sysroot identity from hidden local state into an ecosystem design problem.
- Rust-for-Linux explicitly needs a blessed way to rebuild std together with ABI-affecting and hardening-related compiler flags, and the end-of-2025 program update says other adopters like CPython are surfacing a similar cluster of needs (`build-std`, platform support, sanitizer support).
- The missing contribution is not another wrapper around `-Z build-std`, not another cross-build convenience layer, and not a bespoke org cache. The missing contribution is the portable artifact, activation, and lane-classification layer for **productized Rust toolchain variants**.

**What the contribution should look like in practice**
- Treat [`gaps/toolchain-variants-sysroots-cross-builds-and-sanitizer-support-contracts.md`](./gaps/toolchain-variants-sysroots-cross-builds-and-sanitizer-support-contracts.md), [`design/toolchain-productization-lane-map.md`](./design/toolchain-productization-lane-map.md), [`design/toolchain-productization-stack.md`](./design/toolchain-productization-stack.md), and [`proposals/epic-toolchain-productization-stack.md`](./proposals/epic-toolchain-productization-stack.md) as the synthesis/proposal layer.
- Treat [`design/cross-toolchain-kit.md`](./design/cross-toolchain-kit.md) as the provisioning/handoff layer, [`design/sysroot-pack-kit.md`](./design/sysroot-pack-kit.md) as the stdlib-identity anchor, and [`design/sanitizer-battery-kit.md`](./design/sanitizer-battery-kit.md) as the runtime-analysis consumer that imports runtime/sysroot provenance.
- Use [`design/toolchain-productization-pilot-program.md`](./design/toolchain-productization-pilot-program.md) as the execution anchor.
- The next credible move is a ranked pilot program: instrumented-stdlib lane → hardened / ABI-modifying lane → custom-target / tier-3 lane → shared-cache / CI lane → external-build-system handoff lane.
- The bar is not “make `build-std` easier on my machine”. The bar is “portable provisioning, stdlib identity, activation/reuse, and runtime-analysis evidence that CI, release engineering, firmware, safety/security review, and large nontrivial adopters can actually consume.”

### 17) Wasm Component Kit
**Why it remains strategic**
- Wasm Components are an explicit 2026 flagship.
- The official component-model Rust guide now says native `wasm32-wasip2` tooling is first-class and that `cargo-component` is being deprecated, but the same guide still says custom-WIT bindings need `wit-bindgen`. Meanwhile `cargo-component` remains experimental and still owns important non-WASI/custom-WIT and Warg-publish workflows.
- `wkg` is also getting more real: it already defines shared config and `wkg.lock` files intended for reuse by other language-specific tooling. That means the ecosystem is converging, but not converged.
- This is exactly when a companion contract layer matters most: during transition, not after everything has already flattened into folklore or one tool's incidental outputs.

**What the contribution should look like in practice**
- Treat [`design/wasm-component-kit.md`](./design/wasm-component-kit.md), [`design/wasm-component-pilot-program.md`](./design/wasm-component-pilot-program.md), and [`proposals/epic-wasm-component-kit.md`](./proposals/epic-wasm-component-kit.md) as one execution band.
- Follow a ranked rollout: native Cargo export first, `cargo-component` bridge second, composition/host requirement third, registry/package identity fourth, and runtime/release/policy consumer imports fifth.
- Keep crate identity, WIT/package identity, component artifact identity, and published package identity separate; keep native Cargo and `cargo-component` lanes comparable but non-equivalent; keep browser `wasm-bindgen` workflows out of scope.
- The bar is not “another wrapper around `cargo-component`” or “a giant component platform dashboard.” The bar is “the missing reviewable contract layer for component workflows during a live ecosystem transition.”

### 18) Compatibility Claims Stack (Support Envelope + Acceptance Surface, with Debuggability imports)
**Why this now deserves frontier treatment**
- Official Rust work is making compatibility boundaries more dynamic, not less. Target tiers evolve, runtime baselines shift, docs.rs target defaults change, debugger support varies by debugger/version/OS, and compiler-lane behavior is moving through next-solver and related work.
- The safety-critical adoption writeup makes the missing problem explicit: teams need to map upstream support language to what they can responsibly bet on for a platform and product lifetime.
- The debugging survey and debuginfo documentation make another hidden truth explicit: “supported” is not one thing. A project may compile and run while still lacking stable debugger/version/visualizer/async-debug support.
- The 2025 State of Rust survey says online docs remain canonical while machine consumers rise, which makes attachable compatibility truth more valuable than prose-only caveats.

**What the contribution should look like in practice**
- Treat [`design/compatibility-claims-lane-map.md`](./design/compatibility-claims-lane-map.md), [`design/compatibility-claims-stack.md`](./design/compatibility-claims-stack.md), [`design/compatibility-claims-pilot-program.md`](./design/compatibility-claims-pilot-program.md), and [`proposals/epic-compatibility-claims-stack.md`](./proposals/epic-compatibility-claims-stack.md) as one execution band.
- Support Envelope Kit should own dev-host/source-build/release-artifact/docs/runtime-floor truth and read through [`design/compatibility-claims-lane-map.md`](./design/compatibility-claims-lane-map.md), advancing through [`design/support-envelope-pilot-program.md`](./design/support-envelope-pilot-program.md). Acceptance Surface Kit should own solver-/borrow-check-sensitive pattern catalogs, compiler-lane profiles, workaround truth, and acceptance diffs. Debuggability should export debugger tuple truth into this stack rather than being silently absorbed by it.
- The next credible move is now a thin `cargo compat` / `compat-pack/v0` posture: released-binary support lane first, debugger tuple lane second, advanced-pattern acceptance lane third, mixed-workspace compatibility bundles fourth, and long-lived/safety-oriented support lanes fifth.
- The bar is not “a better matrix page”, “a smarter UI-test runner”, or “one compatibility badge”. The bar is “reviewable compatibility claims that humans, CI, release processes, docs, auditors, and ecosystem tools can all consume honestly,” while preserving platform-support, debugger-support, and acceptance truths as distinct lanes.

### 19) Async Reliability Stack (Async Lifecycle + Replay + DST)
**Why this now belongs on the frontier edge**
- Async remains an explicit ongoing flagship theme: the 2025H1 async flagship already called out runtime choice, runtime interoperability, cancellation sharp edges, and reliability as ecosystem-level pain, and the 2026 flagship themes keep **Just Add Async** active rather than treating async as finished.
- The Async Book is being actively rewritten and now teaches concurrency primitives, structured concurrency, and pinning; its current state-of-async guidance still says runtime compatibility constraints and higher maintenance burden are part of reality. That makes the missing contribution more operational than theoretical.
- Tokio already has credible shutdown building blocks (`CancellationToken`, `TaskTracker`), and the current docs now make child-token propagation and tracker-drain guarantees explicit enough to anchor a lifecycle lane. Meanwhile Loom, Shuttle, Turmoil, and MadSim prove the ecosystem has several serious but non-equivalent exploration families. The missing layer is the ranked contract tying them together without pretending they are the same thing.
- This seam helps the archive rebalance toward runtime/service reliability while still staying grounded in real Rust signals and existing ecosystem practice.

**What the contribution should look like in practice**
- Treat [`design/async-reliability-stack.md`](./design/async-reliability-stack.md), [`design/async-reliability-pilot-program.md`](./design/async-reliability-pilot-program.md), [`proposals/epic-async-reliability-stack.md`](./proposals/epic-async-reliability-stack.md), [`design/async-lifecycle-kit.md`](./design/async-lifecycle-kit.md), [`design/replay-kit.md`](./design/replay-kit.md), and [`design/dst-kit.md`](./design/dst-kit.md) as one coupled frontier band.
- Async Lifecycle should keep owning task ownership, runtime capability profiles, cancellation, shutdown policy, and lifecycle evidence. Replay should keep owning captured failures and exact-vs-best-effort replay posture. DST should keep owning seeds, world models, histories, and exploration reports.
- The next credible move is now explicit: a thin `cargo asyncdoctor` / `async-reliability-pack/v0` layer above lifecycle, replay, and exploration imports, rolled out in rank order as Tokio shutdown truth first, failure handoff to replay second, deterministic simulation attachment third, runtime-portable library posture fourth, and debugger / incident / support consumers fifth.
- The bar is not “a better runtime wrapper”, “one deterministic scheduler”, or “one async dashboard”. The bar is “portable async reliability evidence that keeps lifecycle truth, replay truth, and exploration truth separate while making them composable.”


## Emerging seam just below the async/navigation bands: Async Commons Kit
**Why this now deserves explicit frontier treatment**
- Rust’s March 20, 2026 challenges post is unusually direct that async still feels like a separate programming model for many users and that library choice can immediately lock projects into one runtime family. That is a lower-level ecosystem-seam warning, not only a request for more runtime tutorials.
- The 2025H1 async flagship already said runtime choice and runtime interoperability are core pain points, while the Async Book still says async Rust has compatibility constraints between runtimes and a higher maintenance burden than synchronous Rust. Those are strong signals that the missing contribution is not just more lifecycle guidance but a portability substrate beneath it.
- Rust already has some neutral async vocabulary (`Future` in `std`, `Stream`/`futures-io` in ecosystem crates) and some clearly runtime-shaped surfaces (Tokio I/O, task, timer, and compat layers). The missing layer is the review contract that keeps **neutral async seams, capability requirements, runtime-shaped APIs, adapter lossiness, and blocked/watch seams** distinct instead of forcing each downstream stack to re-invent that map.
- This seam composes directly with **Async Lifecycle**, **Async Reliability**, **Interop Commons**, and **Navigation + Atlas**. It is the cleanest way to turn “async lock-in” from folklore into importable evidence.

**What the contribution should look like in practice**
- Treat [`design/async-commons-kit.md`](./design/async-commons-kit.md), [`design/async-commons-pilot-program.md`](./design/async-commons-pilot-program.md), and [`proposals/epic-async-commons-kit.md`](./proposals/epic-async-commons-kit.md) as one execution band.
- Async Commons should own `async-seam`, `async-capability-profile`, `async-common-surface`, `async-adapter-profile`, `async-vector-set`, `async-readiness-report`, and `async-commons-pack`. Async Lifecycle and Async Reliability should import those artifacts rather than quietly re-stating runtime portability themselves.
- The next credible move is a ranked pilot program: I/O seam first, capability-profile lane second, explicit stream/async-sequence watch-report third, Async Lifecycle consumer import fourth, and Atlas/Adoption consumer import fifth.
- The bar is not “a universal async facade”, “a better runtime wrapper”, or “a badge saying all runtimes work.” The bar is “portable async-commons evidence that names what is truly shared, what is adapter-shaped, what is lossy, and what still needs language/compiler progress.”

### 20) Release Truth Stack (Release Pipeline + Signed Binaries + Repro Build, with Inventory / Distribution handoffs)
**Why this now deserves frontier treatment**
- Rust’s release story is no longer “just publish to crates.io”. Publishing remains permanent and package-oriented, while crates.io Trusted Publishing has become materially stronger and more policyable.
- Rust’s 2026 flagship work keeps **public/private dependencies** and **SBOM support** on the active supply-chain roadmap, and Cargo is growing more explicit artifact / uplift / machine-facing seams. That means “what exactly shipped?” is becoming an ecosystem concern rather than a private CI question.
- The ecosystem already has serious point tools for release PRs, machine-readable artifact manifests, and signed binary installs. The missing contribution is therefore not another wrapper. It is a **portable producer-side release boundary** that ties source publish, artifact publish, signatures, rebuild evidence, and attached inventory truth together without collapsing them into one fake status.

**What the contribution should look like in practice**
- Treat [`design/release-truth-stack.md`](./design/release-truth-stack.md), [`design/release-truth-pilot-program.md`](./design/release-truth-pilot-program.md), [`design/release-pipeline-kit.md`](./design/release-pipeline-kit.md), [`design/release-pipeline-pilot-program.md`](./design/release-pipeline-pilot-program.md), [`design/signed-binaries-kit.md`](./design/signed-binaries-kit.md), [`design/repro-build-kit.md`](./design/repro-build-kit.md), [`design/repro-build-lane-map.md`](./design/repro-build-lane-map.md), [`design/repro-build-pilot-program.md`](./design/repro-build-pilot-program.md), and [`proposals/epic-release-truth-stack.md`](./proposals/epic-release-truth-stack.md) as one coupled execution band.
- Release Pipeline should keep owning release subject / intent / manifest / policy / `release-pack/v0`. Signed Binaries should keep owning `binpack/v0` and `binverify-report/v0`. Repro Build should now explicitly keep owning **source-package reproducibility, final-artifact compare modes, provenance/SBOM attestation attachment posture, offline bundles, lifecycle notes, and `repro-pack/v0`**. Inventory Evidence and Distribution Contract should remain imported handoff lanes rather than hidden sublayers.
